```json
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod42/projects/clkc_mech_interp/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work investigated whether knowledge conflict signals in multilingual LLMs are detectable across languages and whether detected signals causally influence conflict resolution.\nThrough residual stream probing on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B, we showed that conflict signals are linearly detectable both within and across languages, with balanced accuracies of 77.6\\% and 75.0\\% on English within-language detection and all cross-lingual transfer directions significantly exceeding chance (binomial $p < 0.001$).\nThe observed transfer asymmetry (ZH$\\to$EN exceeding EN$\\to$ZH by approximately 7~pp) matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline in magnitude and direction, establishing that this pattern reflects the general cross-lingual representational structure of English-dominant models rather than conflict-specific encoding.\n\nA central finding is the dissociation between detection and decision.\nProbe accuracy peaks at late layers (L28, 88\\% depth), but representation steering succeeds only at a mid-layer (L13, 41\\% depth; Fisher $p = 0.002$), and all late-layer interventions fail.\nThis result demonstrates that linear detectability does not imply causal influence, and that conflict resolution occurs upstream of where the outcome becomes linearly readable.\nFor probing-based intervention research, this dissociation implies that cross-layer sweeps are necessary to identify the computationally relevant site, as targeting the probe peak alone can miss the true decision point.\n\nSeveral directions remain open.\nQwen steering at L14 (the ZH$\\to$ZH peak) and L23 yielded no significant effects, indicating that the detection-decision dissociation observed in Llama does not straightforwardly transfer across architectures.\nFuture work should explore finer-grained layer sweeps and alternative steering methods, evaluate typologically diverse language pairs beyond EN-ZH, and apply sparse autoencoder analysis to decompose conflict representations into interpretable features.\n",
        "numLines": 16,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 16
      }
    },
```

```json
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod42/projects/clkc_mech_interp/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Detection and decision occur at different layers.}\nOur central mechanistic finding is a dissociation between where conflict signals become linearly readable and where they exert causal influence on model behavior.\nIn Llama, probe accuracy on EN$\\to$EN peaks at layer~28 (88\\% depth), yet representation steering succeeds only at layer~13 (41\\% depth; Fisher $p = 0.002$), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant behavioral change ($p > 0.3$).\nThis dissociation has a cross-lingual counterpart: the Llama ZH$\\to$ZH probe peaks at L13, the same layer where steering succeeds.\nMid-layers thus serve a dual role as the site of cross-lingual alignment \\citep{muller2021first, wendler2024llamas} and the locus where conflict signals acquire causal force.\nThe EN$\\to$EN peak at L28 is best understood as a diagnostic readout, a linearly readable trace of a decision that has already been made upstream.\nA practical implication follows: the probe peak layer is not the causal layer, and intervention experiments that target only the highest-accuracy layer risk missing the computationally relevant site.\nCross-layer sweeps, as conducted here, are necessary to locate the true decision point.\nThe analogous Qwen experiments at L14 and L23 produced no significant steering effect and zero directional flips.\nThree non-exclusive explanations apply: (a) Qwen's conflict resolution may not depend on a single linear direction in the residual stream, distributing causal structure across multiple computational paths; (b) the severe class imbalance (9.5\\% parametric baseline, $n = 8$) left the test underpowered to detect moderate effect sizes; and (c) the causal layer in Qwen may lie at a depth not tested.\nUntil a balanced replication is conducted, cross-model generalization of the detection-decision dissociation remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Conflict signals are encoded in a general cross-lingual space.}\nThe XNLI baseline comparison reveals that the ZH$\\to$EN transfer advantage ($+6.0$ and $+6.7$~pp for Llama and Qwen) closely matches the conflict task asymmetry ($+7.3$ and $+7.2$~pp for Llama and Qwen), with substantially overlapping confidence intervals.\nThe asymmetry is therefore not conflict-specific; conflict detection piggybacks on the model's pre-existing cross-lingual alignment structure.\nThis interpretation connects to the linguistic affinity effects reported by \\citet{zhao2026when}: the CLEAR benchmark found that conflict resolution preferences vary by language pair, a pattern that may inherit from the quality of underlying representational alignment documented by \\citet{kargaran2024mexa} and \\citet{wendler2024llamas}.\nAny cross-lingual probing study must account for this baseline asymmetry before attributing transfer differences to task-specific phenomena.\n\n\\paragraph{Relation to interpretability without actionability.}\n\\citet{basu2026interpretability} reported that steering failed in clinical NLP tasks despite near-perfect probe accuracy (98.2\\% AUROC).\nOur results present a partial contrast: mid-layer steering in knowledge conflict tasks succeeds with a weaker probe signal.\nA key difference is layer selection.\n\\citeauthor{basu2026interpretability} evaluated steering at layers selected for probe accuracy; our analysis shows that the layer with highest probe accuracy (L28) also fails to steer, and causal influence is found only at L13.\nTask characteristics may also contribute: knowledge conflict resolution involves a discrete choice between two competing answer sources, which may concentrate causal structure at a single computational stage.\nWe frame this as a difference in causal influence rather than full causal control, since steering shifts resolution probabilities without fully determining the outcome.\n\n\\paragraph{A suggestive MLP trend.}\nWhen MLP sublayer representations replace full residual stream inputs for EN$\\to$ZH probes, the conflict task shows positive MLP gains (Qwen $+3.84$~pp, Llama $+5.21$~pp), while the XNLI baseline yields negative or near-zero gains.\nHowever, bootstrap confidence intervals include zero for both models, so this difference does not reach statistical significance.\nWe report it as a suggestive trend: if confirmed with larger samples or additional models, it would indicate that conflict-specific information has a nonlinear component beyond what general semantic representations capture.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and forward look.}\nOur analysis covers one language pair (EN-ZH), two models, and confirmed steering effects in Llama only.\nThese constraints, along with dataset and task limitations, are discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}.\nExtending the steering analysis with balanced test sets across additional architectures and typologically diverse language pairs would test whether the detection-decision dissociation generalizes beyond the configurations studied here.\n",
        "numLines": 39,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 39
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/discussion.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "85953b19-2ce5-4e4a-87fb-9907715c3085",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod42/projects/clkc_mech_interp/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Models.}\nWe probe two instruction-tuned models with comparable parameter counts but distinct architectures: Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (32 layers, hidden dimension 4096) \\citep{grattafiori2024llama} and Qwen3-8B (36 layers, hidden dimension 4096) \\citep{yang2024qwen25}.\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset.}\nWe use ConflictQA \\citep{xie2024adaptive}, a benchmark of context-parametric knowledge conflicts where a provided context contradicts the model's parametric knowledge.\nAfter verifying that each model can correctly answer the parametric-knowledge question without context, we retain 1{,}368 samples for Llama (1{,}096 train / 272 test) and 966 samples for Qwen (773 train / 193 test), split 80/20 with stratification.\n\n\\paragraph{Translation.}\nWe translate all English contexts and questions into Chinese using a Qwen3-8B translation pipeline (EN$\\to$ZH), with quality validated by COMET \\citep{rei2020comet} back-translation scores.\n\n\\paragraph{Probing.}\nFor each layer $l$, we train a logistic regression probe on the last-token residual stream representation $\\mathbf{h}_l$, with binary labels indicating whether the model chose the contextual or parametric answer.\nWe report balanced accuracy to account for class imbalance.\nAll confidence intervals (CIs) are computed via bootstrap resampling with paired differences for asymmetry estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-conflict baseline.}\nTo test whether cross-lingual transfer patterns are specific to knowledge conflicts, we run the same probing pipeline on XNLI \\citep{conneau2018xnli}, classifying entailment versus contradiction.\n\n\\subsection{Within-Language Conflict Detection}\n\\label{sec:within_lang}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports balanced accuracy for all probe transfer directions.\nBoth models encode linearly detectable conflict signals in their residual streams.\nLlama achieves 77.6\\% on EN$\\to$EN (peak at layer 28, 88\\% depth) and 72.1\\% on ZH$\\to$ZH.\nQwen reaches 75.0\\% on EN$\\to$EN (peak at layer 24, 67\\% depth) and 72.6\\% on ZH$\\to$ZH.\nAll within-language accuracies are significantly above chance (binomial $p < 0.001$).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:layer_probe} shows the layer-wise probe accuracy curves for both models.\nThese results confirm that knowledge conflict signals are linearly separable in the residual stream across both architectures and both languages.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Lingual Transfer and Asymmetry}\n\\label{sec:cross_lingual}\n\nAll four cross-lingual transfer directions achieve balanced accuracy above chance (binomial $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), confirming that conflict representations partially overlap across languages.\nBoth models show a consistent directional trend: probes trained on Chinese representations transfer to English (ZH$\\to$EN) more accurately than the reverse direction (EN$\\to$ZH).\nFor Llama, $\\Delta_{\\text{asym}} = +7.3$ percentage points (pp) with a 95\\% bootstrap CI of [0.4, 14.0] ($p = 0.038$); for Qwen, $\\Delta_{\\text{asym}} = +7.2$ pp with CI [$-$0.6, 13.7].\nThe magnitude of this asymmetry closely matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:xnli_baseline}), and the consistent direction across architectures suggests a shared structural property rather than a model-specific artifact (Figure~\\ref{fig:asymmetry_forest}).\n\n%% Table 1: Main cross-lingual probe results\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Single-seed balanced accuracy (\\%) at each direction's optimal layer. $\\Delta_{\\text{asym}}$ and 95\\% CIs from paired bootstrap ($n = 2{,}000$ resamples). Bold: best per-task direction.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nDirection & Llama-3.1-8B & Qwen3-8B \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{Conflict Task}} \\\\\nEN $\\to$ EN & \\textbf{77.6} & \\textbf{75.0} \\\\\nZH $\\to$ ZH & 72.1 & 72.6 \\\\\nEN $\\to$ ZH & 59.6 & 67.3 \\\\\nZH $\\to$ EN & 70.6 & 73.2 \\\\\n$\\Delta_{\\text{asym}}$ & +7.3 [0.4, 14.0] & +7.2 [$-$0.6, 13.7] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{XNLI Baseline}} \\\\\nEN $\\to$ EN & 94.0 & 96.6 \\\\\nZH $\\to$ ZH & 87.5 & 89.5 \\\\\nEN $\\to$ ZH & 89.0 & 90.5 \\\\\nZH $\\to$ EN & \\textbf{94.9} & \\textbf{97.2} \\\\\n$\\Delta_{\\text{asym}}$ & +6.0 [0.9, 11.3] & +6.7 [2.4, 11.7] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Non-Conflict Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:xnli_baseline}\n\nIf the ZH$\\to$EN transfer advantage were specific to knowledge conflicts, it should be absent or substantially reduced in a non-conflict semantic task.\nWe test this prediction using XNLI entailment-versus-contradiction probes.\nBoth models achieve high within-language accuracy on XNLI (Llama 94.0\\% EN, 87.5\\% ZH; Qwen 96.6\\% EN, 89.5\\% ZH), confirming that the probing pipeline captures semantic distinctions beyond knowledge conflicts.\nThe XNLI probes exhibit the same directional asymmetry: Llama $\\Delta_{\\text{asym}} = +6.0$ pp, CI [0.9, 11.3]; Qwen $\\Delta_{\\text{asym}} = +6.7$ pp, CI [2.4, 11.7].\nBoth XNLI CIs exclude zero, and both overlap substantially with the corresponding conflict-task CIs (Figure~\\ref{fig:asymmetry_forest}).\nThe close match in asymmetry magnitude between conflict probes (Llama +7.2, Qwen +7.2 pp) and XNLI probes (Llama +6.0, Qwen +6.7 pp) indicates that the ZH$\\to$EN transfer advantage is not specific to knowledge conflict representations.\nInstead, it reflects a general cross-lingual property: Chinese-trained probes transfer to English more readily than the reverse, regardless of the downstream task.\n\n\\subsection{Confound Exclusion}\n\\label{sec:confounds}\n\nTwo potential confounds could inflate cross-lingual probe accuracy: translation quality and lexical overlap between English and Chinese inputs.\n\n\\paragraph{Translation quality.}\nWe stratify test samples by COMET score into terciles (high, medium, low) and compare EN$\\to$ZH probe accuracy across strata.\nIf translation quality drove probe transfer, high-COMET samples would yield higher accuracy.\nTable~\\ref{tab:confound} shows no such pattern: Qwen exhibits a reversed direction ($\\Delta = -5.6$ pp, Fisher $p = 0.695$), and Llama shows a negligible effect ($\\Delta = +1.7$ pp, Fisher $p = 0.761$).\nTranslation quality does not confound the cross-lingual transfer results.\n\n\\paragraph{Token overlap.}\nPer-sample Jaccard similarity between English and Chinese tokenized inputs averages 3--4\\%, consistent with the minimal lexical overlap between the two languages.\nNeither model shows a positive correlation between token overlap and probe accuracy: Qwen yields no significant correlation ($p > 0.4$), and Llama shows a weak negative correlation ($r = -0.15$, $p = 0.012$), where higher overlap is associated with \\emph{lower} accuracy.\nShared tokens do not drive cross-lingual transfer.\n\n%% Table 2: Confound exclusion\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Confound exclusion analysis. COMET tercile: $\\Delta$ = balanced accuracy difference between high and low COMET score terciles for EN$\\to$ZH probes (Fisher's exact test). Token overlap: Pearson correlation between per-sample Jaccard similarity and EN$\\to$ZH probe accuracy.}\n\\label{tab:confound}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrl}\n\\toprule\nConfound & Model & Result & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCOMET tercile & Qwen & $\\Delta = -5.6$ pp & 0.695 \\\\\nCOMET tercile & Llama & $\\Delta = +1.7$ pp & 0.761 \\\\\n\\midrule\nToken overlap & Qwen & n.s. & $>$0.4 \\\\\nToken overlap & Llama & $r = -0.15$ & 0.012 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Causal Evidence via Representation Steering}\n\\label{sec:steering}\n\nThe probing results establish that conflict signals are detectable, but detection does not imply causal relevance.\nWe test whether the probe weight direction $\\mathbf{w}_{\\text{probe}}$ causally influences conflict resolution by injecting $\\alpha \\cdot \\mathbf{w}_{\\text{probe}}$ into the last-token residual stream at a target layer during inference.\nWe measure the flip rate (fraction of samples that switch answer category) and compare against a random-direction control of equal magnitude via Fisher's exact test.\n\n\\paragraph{L13 (mid-layer, 41\\% depth).}\nSteering at layer 13 produces significantly more behavioral change than random controls in both directions (Table~\\ref{tab:steering}).\nPositive steering ($\\alpha = +5$) yields a flip rate of 19.9\\% versus 6.6\\% for the random control (Fisher $p = 0.002$), with all 4 directional flips moving from parametric to contextual answers.\nNegative steering ($\\alpha = -20$) yields 36.0\\% versus 17.6\\% (Fisher $p < 0.001$), with 5 of 6 directional flips moving from contextual to parametric.\nBoth results survive Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons.\nThe bidirectionality confirms that the probe weight vector encodes a meaningful axis of conflict resolution: positive perturbations push toward contextual answers, and negative perturbations push toward parametric answers.\n\n\\paragraph{Negative layers.}\nSteering at L28 (the probing peak layer, 88\\% depth) produces no significant effect with either last-token or all-position injection (all Fisher $p > 0.3$; Table~\\ref{tab:steering}).\nL18 (the MLP probe peak, 56\\% depth) also shows no causal influence (all Fisher $p > 0.44$).\n\n\\paragraph{$\\alpha = +20$ reversal.}\nAt extreme perturbation magnitude ($\\alpha = +20$), the control condition produces more flips than the steering condition ($p = 0.053$).\nThis indicates coherence collapse at large perturbation magnitudes, constraining the effective steering range.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection $\\neq$ Decision.}\nIn Llama, the detectable conflict signal peaks at late layers (L28, 88\\% depth), but causal influence on conflict resolution is localized to the mid-layer (L13, 41\\% depth).\nProbing accuracy and causal relevance thus identify different computational stages: late layers encode the outcome of conflict resolution in a linearly readable form, while mid-layers host the computation that determines the outcome.\nInjecting at all token positions (L13, all) yields identical results to last-token injection, indicating that the causal signal is concentrated in the final token's residual stream.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model steering.}\nSteering at Qwen's mid-layer (L14, 39\\% depth; probe accuracy 70.2\\%) produces no significant behavioral change (best Fisher $p = 0.682$, $\\alpha = -10$), with zero directional flips across all steering magnitudes ($n_{\\text{test}} = 84$).\nL23 (64\\% depth; probe accuracy 73.2\\%) is similarly negative (all $p = 1.0$).\nDespite comparable probe accuracies (70--73\\%), the Qwen probe directions fail to causally shift answer routing, indicating that linear detectability does not entail causal relevance.\nThe mid-layer causal node identified in Llama L13 does not transfer across architectures, suggesting that the locus of conflict resolution may be model-specific even when the representational signature is shared.\n\n%% Table 3: Steering results\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Representation steering results. Flip rate: fraction of test samples switching answer category. Control: random-direction perturbation of equal magnitude. Significant results ($p < 0.05$) in bold.$^{\\dagger}$}\n\\label{tab:steering}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Depth & $\\alpha$ & Steer (\\%) & Ctrl (\\%) & Fisher $p$ & Dir. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Llama-3.1-8B}} \\\\\n\\textbf{L13} & \\textbf{41\\%} & $\\mathbf{+5}$ & \\textbf{19.9} & \\textbf{6.6} & $\\mathbf{0.002}$ & \\textbf{4 p$\\to$c} \\\\\n\\textbf{L13} & \\textbf{41\\%} & $\\mathbf{-20}$ & \\textbf{36.0} & \\textbf{17.6} & $\\mathbf{<0.001}$ & \\textbf{5 c$\\to$p} \\\\\nL13 (all) & 41\\% & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textit{identical to last-token}} & \\\\\nL18 & 56\\% & $+2$ & 3.7 & 1.5 & 0.447 & \\\\\nL28 & 88\\% & $-20$ & 14.7 & 16.9 & 0.740 & \\\\\nL28 (all) & 88\\% & $-20$ & 11.8 & 11.8 & 1.000 & \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Qwen3-8B}} \\\\\nL14 & 39\\% & $-10$ & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{0 dir.\\ flips} & 0.682 & \\\\\nL23 & 64\\% & --- & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{0 dir.\\ flips} & 1.000 & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\begin{flushleft}\n\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$ At $\\alpha = +20$ (Llama L13), the control flip rate exceeds steering ($p = 0.053$), indicating coherence collapse at extreme perturbation magnitudes.\n\\end{flushleft}\n\\end{table}\n",
        "numLines": 175,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 175
      }
    },
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012kS1BS4crb3eyt9UB6Cvsw`</summary>

```text
148	\paragraph{$\alpha = +20$ reversal.}
149	At extreme perturbation magnitude ($\alpha = +20$), the control condition produces more flips than the steering condition ($p = 0.053$).
150	This indicates coherence collapse at large perturbation magnitudes, constraining the effective steering range.
151	
152	\paragraph{Detection $\neq$ Decision.}
153	The detectable conflict signal peaks at late layers (L28, 88\% depth), but causal influence on conflict resolution is localized to the mid-layer (L13, 41\% depth).
154	Probing accuracy and causal relevance thus identify different computational stages: late layers encode the outcome of conflict resolution in a linearly readable form, while mid-layers host the computation that determines the outcome.
155	
156	\paragraph{Cross-model note.}
157	The analogous Qwen steering experiments at L14 (the ZH$\to$ZH probe peak, 39\% depth) and L23 (64\% depth) did not reach significance at any $\alpha$ value (best Fisher $p = 0.682$ at L14; all $p = 1.0$ at L23), with zero directional flips (p$\to$c or c$\to$p) across all conditions (Table~\ref{tab:steering}).
158	However, this test was substantially underpowered: only 9.5\% of Qwen test samples resolved to the parametric answer at baseline ($n = 8$), compared to 23.5\% for Llama ($n = 32$), leaving insufficient parametric-class samples to detect directional shifts.
159	The non-significant Qwen result is therefore uninformative about whether the mid-layer causal effect generalizes across architectures; a balanced test set would be required to draw conclusions.
160	
161	\begin{table}[t]
162	\centering
163	\caption{Representation steering results. Flip rate: fraction of test samples switching answer category. Control: random-direction perturbation of equal magnitude. Significant results ($p < 0.05$) in bold.$^{\dagger}$}
164	\label{tab:steering}
165	\small
166	\begin{tabular}{lcrrrrl}
167	\toprule
168	Layer & Depth & $\alpha$ & Steer (\%) & Ctrl (\%) & Fisher $p$ & Dir. \\
169	\midrule
170	\textbf{L13} & \textbf{41\%} & $\mathbf{+5}$ & \textbf{19.9} & \textbf{6.6} & $\mathbf{0.002}$ & \textbf{4 p$\to$c} \\
171	\textbf{L13} & \textbf{41\%} & $\mathbf{-20}$ & \textbf{36.0} & \textbf{17.6} & $\mathbf{<0.001}$ & \textbf{5 c$\to$p} \\
172	L18 & 56\% & $+2$ & 3.7 & 1.5 & 0.447 & \\
173	L28 & 88\% & $-20$ & 14.7 & 16.9 & 0.740 & \\
174	L28 (all) & 88\% & $-20$ & 11.8 & 11.8 & 1.000 & \\
175	\midrule
176	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Qwen3-8B ($n_{\text{test}} = 84$; 9.5\% parametric baseline)}} \\
177	L14 & 39\% & $-10$ & \multicolumn{2}{c}{n.s.} & 0.682 & 0 dir. \\
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DC3s7bsKyr88usEnZeHF3S`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our study has three main limitations.
5	First, we evaluate only one language pair (English-Chinese) and two model architectures (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B).
6	While the consistent patterns across these two architecturally distinct models are encouraging, typologically diverse language pairs (e.g., English-German, English-Japanese) and additional model families are needed to assess the generality of our findings.
7	Second, the causal steering effect is confirmed only in Llama.
8	The Qwen steering test did not reach significance, but was underpowered due to class imbalance: only 9.5\% of Qwen test samples resolved to the parametric answer at baseline (8 samples), compared to 23.5\% for Llama (32 samples).
9	Until the detection-decision dissociation is confirmed in a second architecture with balanced test data, its generality remains provisional.
10	Third, the effective dataset size (966 to 1,368 samples per model after parametric knowledge verification) is constrained by the requirement that each model must correctly answer the question without context.
11	This verification step is necessary to ensure genuine knowledge conflicts, but it limits the statistical power of our analyses, particularly for stratified comparisons such as the COMET tercile ablation.
12	
```

</details>

```text
44	\subsection{Cross-Lingual Transfer and Asymmetry}
45	\label{sec:cross_lingual}
46	
47	All four cross-lingual transfer directions achieve balanced accuracy above chance (binomial $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), confirming that conflict representations partially overlap across languages.
48	Both models show a consistent directional trend: probes trained on Chinese representations transfer to English (ZH$\to$EN) more accurately than the reverse direction (EN$\to$ZH).
49	For Llama, $\Delta_{\text{asym}} = +7.3$ percentage points (pp) with a 95\% bootstrap CI of [0.4, 14.0] ($p = 0.038$); for Qwen, $\Delta_{\text{asym}} = +7.2$ pp with CI [$-$0.6, 13.7].
50	The magnitude of this asymmetry closely matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline (\S\ref{sec:xnli_baseline}), and the consistent direction across architectures suggests a shared structural property rather than a model-specific artifact (Figure~\ref{fig:asymmetry_forest}).
51
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Detection and decision occur at different layers.}
5	Our central mechanistic finding is a dissociation between where conflict signals become linearly readable and where they exert causal influence on model behavior.
6	In Llama, probe accuracy on EN$\to$EN peaks at layer~28 (88\% depth), yet representation steering succeeds only at layer~13 (41\% depth; Fisher $p = 0.002$), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant behavioral change ($p > 0.3$).
7	This dissociation has a cross-lingual counterpart: the Llama ZH$\to$ZH probe peaks at L13, the same layer where steering succeeds.
8	Mid-layers thus serve a dual role as the site of cross-lingual alignment \citep{muller2021first, wendler2024llamas} and the locus where conflict signals acquire causal force.
9	The EN$\to$EN peak at L28 is best understood as a diagnostic readout, a linearly readable trace of a decision that has already been made upstream.
10	A practical implication follows: the probe peak layer is not the causal layer, and intervention experiments that target only the highest-accuracy layer risk missing the computationally relevant site.
11	Cross-layer sweeps, as conducted here, are necessary to locate the true decision point.
12	
13	\paragraph{Conflict signals are encoded in a general cross-lingual space.}
14	The XNLI baseline comparison reveals that the ZH$\to$EN transfer advantage ($+6.0$ and $+6.7$~pp for Llama and Qwen) closely matches the conflict task asymmetry ($+7.2$~pp for both models), with substantially overlapping confidence intervals.
15	The asymmetry is therefore not conflict-specific; conflict detection piggybacks on the model's pre-existing cross-lingual alignment structure.
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Detection and decision occur at different layers.}\nOur central mechanistic finding is a dissociation between where conflict signals become linearly readable and where they exert causal influence on model behavior.\nIn Llama, probe accuracy on EN$\\to$EN peaks at layer~28 (88\\% depth), yet representation steering succeeds only at layer~13 (41\\% depth; Fisher $p = 0.002$), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant behavioral change ($p > 0.3$).\nThis dissociation has a cross-lingual counterpart: the Llama ZH$\\to$ZH probe peaks at L13, the same layer where steering succeeds.\nMid-layers thus serve a dual role as the site of cross-lingual alignment \\citep{muller2021first, wendler2024llamas} and the locus where conflict signals acquire causal force.\nThe EN$\\to$EN peak at L28 is best understood as a diagnostic readout, a linearly readable trace of a decision that has already been made upstream.\nA practical implication follows: the probe peak layer is not the causal layer, and intervention experiments that target only the highest-accuracy layer risk missing the computationally relevant site.\nCross-layer sweeps, as conducted here, are necessary to locate the true decision point.\n\n\\paragraph{Conflict signals are encoded in a general cross-lingual space.}\nThe XNLI baseline comparison reveals that the ZH$\\to$EN transfer advantage ($+6.0$ and $+6.7$~pp for Llama and Qwen) closely matches the conflict task asymmetry ($+7.2$~pp for both models), with substantially overlapping confidence intervals.\nThe asymmetry is therefore not conflict-specific; conflict detection piggybacks on the model's pre-existing cross-lingual alignment structure.",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 36
    }
  },
```

### Tool result

_source line 1831 · 2026-05-22T16:46:50.388Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UVwX5JmXet55nJucR6CDsz`</summary>

```text
10	A practical implication follows: the probe peak layer is not the causal layer, and intervention experiments that target only the highest-accuracy layer risk missing the computationally relevant site.
11	Cross-layer sweeps, as conducted here, are necessary to locate the true decision point.
12	
13	\paragraph{Conflict signals are encoded in a general cross-lingual space.}
14	The XNLI baseline comparison reveals that the ZH$\to$EN transfer advantage ($+6.0$ and $+6.7$~pp for Llama and Qwen) closely matches the conflict task asymmetry ($+7.2$~pp for both models), with substantially overlapping confidence intervals.
15	The asymmetry is therefore not conflict-specific; conflict detection piggybacks on the model's pre-existing cross-lingual alignment structure.
16	This interpretation connects to the linguistic affinity effects reported by \citet{zhao2026when}: the CLEAR benchmark found that conflict resolution preferences vary by language pair, a pattern that may inherit from the quality of underlying representational alignment documented by \citet{kargaran2024mexa} and \citet{wendler2024llamas}.
17	Any cross-lingual probing study must account for this baseline asymmetry before attributing transfer differences to task-specific phenomena.
18	
19	\paragraph{Relation to interpretability without actionability.}
20	\citet{basu2026interpretability} reported that steering failed in clinical NLP tasks despite near-perfect probe accuracy (98.2\% AUROC).
21	Our results present a partial contrast: mid-layer steering in knowledge conflict tasks succeeds with a weaker probe signal.
22	A key difference is layer selection.
23	\citeauthor{basu2026interpretability} evaluated steering at layers selected for probe accuracy; our analysis shows that the layer with highest probe accuracy (L28) also fails to steer, and causal influence is found only at L13.
24	Task characteristics may also contribute: knowledge conflict resolution involves a discrete choice between two competing answer sources, which may concentrate causal structure at a single computational stage.
25	We frame this as a difference in causal influence rather than full causal control, since steering shifts resolution probabilities without fully determining the outcome.
26	
27	\paragraph{A suggestive MLP trend.}
28	When MLP sublayer representations replace full residual stream inputs for EN$\to$ZH probes, the conflict task shows positive MLP gains (Qwen $+3.84$~pp, Llama $+5.21$~pp), while the XNLI baseline yields negative or near-zero gains.
29	However, bootstrap confidence intervals include zero for both models, so this difference does not reach statistical significance.
30	We report it as a suggestive trend: if confirmed with larger samples or additional models, it would indicate that conflict-specific information has a nonlinear component beyond what general semantic representations capture.
31	
32	\paragraph{Scope and forward look.}
33	Our analysis covers one language pair (EN-ZH), two models, and steering experiments on Llama only.
34	These constraints, along with dataset and task limitations, are discussed in \S\ref{sec:limitations}.
35	Extending the steering analysis to Qwen and to typologically diverse language pairs would test whether the detection-decision dissociation generalizes beyond the configurations studied here.
36	
```

</details>

```json
{
```
```json
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod42/projects/clkc_mech_interp/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
```
```json
  "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Detection and decision occur at different layers.}\nOur central mechanistic finding is a dissociation between where conflict signals become linearly readable and where they exert causal influence on model behavior.\nIn Llama, probe accuracy on EN$\\to$EN peaks at layer~28 (88\\% depth), yet representation steering succeeds only at layer~13 (41\\% depth; Fisher $p = 0.002$), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant behavioral change ($p > 0.3$).\nThis dissociation has a cross-lingual counterpart: the Llama ZH$\\to$ZH probe peaks at L13, the same layer where steering succeeds.\nMid-layers thus serve a dual role as the site of cross-lingual alignment \\citep{muller2021first, wendler2024llamas} and the locus where conflict signals acquire causal force.\nThe EN$\\to$EN peak at L28 is best understood as a diagnostic readout, a linearly readable trace of a decision that has already been made upstream.\nA practical implication follows: the probe peak layer is not the causal layer, and intervention experiments that target only the highest-accuracy layer risk missing the computationally relevant site.\nCross-layer sweeps, as conducted here, are necessary to locate the true decision point.\nThe analogous Qwen experiments at L14 and L23 produced no significant steering effect and zero directional flips.\nThree non-exclusive explanations apply: (a) Qwen's conflict resolution may not depend on a single linear direction in the residual stream, distributing causal structure across multiple computational paths; (b) the severe class imbalance (9.5\\% parametric baseline, $n = 8$) left the test underpowered to detect moderate effect sizes; and (c) the causal layer in Qwen may lie at a depth not tested.\nUntil a balanced replication is conducted, cross-model generalization of the detection-decision dissociation remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Conflict signals are encoded in a general cross-lingual space.}\nThe XNLI baseline comparison reveals that the ZH$\\to$EN transfer advantage ($+6.0$ and $+6.7$~pp for Llama and Qwen) closely matches the conflict task asymmetry ($+7.3$ and $+7.2$~pp for Llama and Qwen), with substantially overlapping confidence intervals.\nThe asymmetry is therefore not conflict-specific; conflict detection piggybacks on the model's pre-existing cross-lingual alignment structure.\nThis interpretation connects to the linguistic affinity effects reported by \\citet{zhao2026when}: the CLEAR benchmark found that conflict resolution preferences vary by language pair, a pattern that may inherit from the quality of underlying representational alignment documented by \\citet{kargaran2024mexa} and \\citet{wendler2024llamas}.\nAny cross-lingual probing study must account for this baseline asymmetry before attributing transfer differences to task-specific phenomena.\n\n\\paragraph{Relation to interpretability without actionability.}\n\\citet{basu2026interpretability} reported that steering failed in clinical NLP tasks despite near-perfect probe accuracy (98.2\\% AUROC).\nOur results present a partial contrast: mid-layer steering in knowledge conflict tasks succeeds with a weaker probe signal.\nA key difference is layer selection.\n\\citeauthor{basu2026interpretability} evaluated steering at layers selected for probe accuracy; our analysis shows that the layer with highest probe accuracy (L28) also fails to steer, and causal influence is found only at L13.\nTask characteristics may also contribute: knowledge conflict resolution involves a discrete choice between two competing answer sources, which may concentrate causal structure at a single computational stage.\nWe frame this as a difference in causal influence rather than full causal control, since steering shifts resolution probabilities without fully determining the outcome.\n\n\\paragraph{A suggestive MLP trend.}\nWhen MLP sublayer representations replace full residual stream inputs for EN$\\to$ZH probes, the conflict task shows positive MLP gains (Qwen $+3.84$~pp, Llama $+5.21$~pp), while the XNLI baseline yields negative or near-zero gains.\nHowever, bootstrap confidence intervals include zero for both models, so this difference does not reach statistical significance.\nWe report it as a suggestive trend: if confirmed with larger samples or additional models, it would indicate that conflict-specific information has a nonlinear component beyond what general semantic representations capture.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and forward look.}\nOur analysis covers one language pair (EN-ZH), two models, and confirmed steering effects in Llama only.\nThese constraints, along with dataset and task limitations, are discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}.\nExtending the steering analysis with balanced test sets across additional architectures and typologically diverse language pairs would test whether the detection-decision dissociation generalizes beyond the configurations studied here.\n"
}
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	\paragraph{Models.}
8	We probe two instruction-tuned models with comparable parameter counts but distinct architectures: Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (32 layers, hidden dimension 4096) \citep{grattafiori2024llama} and Qwen3-8B (36 layers, hidden dimension 4096) \citep{yang2024qwen25}.
9	
10	\paragraph{Dataset.}
11	We use ConflictQA \citep{xie2024adaptive}, a benchmark of context-parametric knowledge conflicts where a provided context contradicts the model's parametric knowledge.
12	After verifying that each model can correctly answer the parametric-knowledge question without context, we retain 1{,}368 samples for Llama (1{,}096 train / 272 test) and 966 samples for Qwen (773 train / 193 test), split 80/20 with stratification.
13	
14	\paragraph{Translation.}
15	We translate all English contexts and questions into Chinese using a Qwen3-8B translation pipeline (EN$\to$ZH), with quality validated by COMET \citep{rei2020comet} back-translation scores.
16	
17	\paragraph{Probing.}
18	For each layer $l$, we train a logistic regression probe on the last-token residual stream representation $\mathbf{h}_l$, with binary labels indicating whether the model chose the contextual or parametric answer.
19	We report balanced accuracy to account for class imbalance.
20	All confidence intervals (CIs) are computed via bootstrap resampling with paired differences for asymmetry estimation.
21	
22	\paragraph{Non-conflict baseline.}
23	To test whether cross-lingual transfer patterns are specific to knowledge conflicts, we run the same probing pipeline on XNLI \citep{conneau2018xnli}, classifying entailment versus contradiction.
24	
25	\subsection{Within-Language Conflict Detection}
26	\label{sec:within_lang}
27	
28	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports balanced accuracy for all probe transfer directions.
29	Both models encode linearly detectable conflict signals in their residual streams.
30	Llama achieves 77.6\% on EN$\to$EN (peak at layer 28, 88\% depth) and 72.1\% on ZH$\to$ZH.
31	Qwen reaches 75.0\% on EN$\to$EN (peak at layer 24, 67\% depth) and 72.6\% on ZH$\to$ZH.
32	All within-language accuracies are significantly above chance (binomial $p < 0.001$).
33	Figure~\ref{fig:layer_probe} shows the layer-wise probe accuracy curves for both models.
34	These results confirm that knowledge conflict signals are linearly separable in the residual stream across both architectures and both languages.
35	
36	%% Figure 2: Layer-wise probe accuracy
37	\begin{figure}[t]
38	\centering
39	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_layerwise_probes.pdf}
40	\caption{Layer-wise probe balanced accuracy for Llama-3.1-8B (left) and Qwen3-8B (right). Solid lines: residual stream probes; dashed lines: MLP sublayer probes. Stars and diamonds mark peak layers. The vertical annotation at L13 marks the layer where representation steering produces significant causal influence (Fisher $p = 0.002$), which coincides with the Llama ZH$\to$ZH probe peak but not the EN$\to$EN peak (L28).}
41	\label{fig:layer_probe}
42	\end{figure}
43	
44	\subsection{Cross-Lingual Transfer and Asymmetry}
45	\label{sec:cross_lingual}
46	
47	All four cross-lingual transfer directions achieve balanced accuracy above chance (binomial $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), confirming that conflict representations partially overlap across languages.
48	Both models show a consistent directional trend: probes trained on Chinese representations transfer to English (ZH$\to$EN) more accurately than the reverse direction (EN$\to$ZH).
49	For Llama, $\Delta_{\text{asym}} = +7.3$ percentage points (pp) with a 95\% bootstrap CI of [0.4, 14.0] ($p = 0.038$); for Qwen, $\Delta_{\text{asym}} = +7.2$ pp with CI [$-$0.6, 13.7].
50	The magnitude of this asymmetry closely matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline (\S\ref{sec:xnli_baseline}), and the consistent direction across architectures suggests a shared structural property rather than a model-specific artifact (Figure~\ref{fig:asymmetry_forest}).
51	
52	\begin{table}[t]
53	\centering
54	\caption{Single-seed balanced accuracy (\%) at each direction's optimal layer. $\Delta_{\text{asym}}$ and 95\% CIs from paired bootstrap ($n = 2{,}000$ resamples). Bold: best per-task direction.}
55	\label{tab:main_results}
56	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
57	\toprule
58	Direction & Llama-3.1-8B & Qwen3-8B \\
59	\midrule
60	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Conflict Task}} \\
61	EN $\to$ EN & \textbf{77.6} & \textbf{75.0} \\
62	ZH $\to$ ZH & 72.1 & 72.6 \\
63	EN $\to$ ZH & 59.6 & 67.3 \\
64	ZH $\to$ EN & 70.6 & 73.2 \\
65	$\Delta_{\text{asym}}$ & +7.3 [0.4, 14.0] & +7.2 [$-$0.6, 13.7] \\
66	\midrule
67	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{XNLI Baseline}} \\
68	EN $\to$ EN & 94.0 & 96.6 \\
69	ZH $\to$ ZH & 87.5 & 89.5 \\
70	EN $\to$ ZH & 89.0 & 90.5 \\
71	ZH $\to$ EN & \textbf{94.9} & \textbf{97.2} \\
72	$\Delta_{\text{asym}}$ & +6.0 [0.9, 11.3] & +6.7 [2.4, 11.7] \\
73	\bottomrule
74	\end{tabular}
75	\end{table}
76	
77	\subsection{Non-Conflict Baseline Comparison}
78	\label{sec:xnli_baseline}
79	
80	If the ZH$\to$EN transfer advantage were specific to knowledge conflicts, it should be absent or substantially reduced in a non-conflict semantic task.
81	We test this prediction using XNLI entailment-versus-contradiction probes.
82	Both models achieve high within-language accuracy on XNLI (Llama 94.0\% EN, 87.5\% ZH; Qwen 96.6\% EN, 89.5\% ZH), confirming that the probing pipeline captures semantic distinctions beyond knowledge conflicts.
83	The XNLI probes exhibit the same directional asymmetry: Llama $\Delta_{\text{asym}} = +6.0$ pp, CI [0.9, 11.3]; Qwen $\Delta_{\text{asym}} = +6.7$ pp, CI [2.4, 11.7].
84	Both XNLI CIs exclude zero, and both overlap substantially with the corresponding conflict-task CIs (Figure~\ref{fig:asymmetry_forest}).
85	The close match in asymmetry magnitude between conflict probes (Llama +7.3, Qwen +7.2 pp) and XNLI probes (Llama +6.0, Qwen +6.7 pp) indicates that the ZH$\to$EN transfer advantage is not specific to knowledge conflict representations.
86	Instead, it reflects a general cross-lingual property: Chinese-trained probes transfer to English more readily than the reverse, regardless of the downstream task.
87	
88	%% Figure 3: Asymmetry forest plot
89	\begin{figure}[t]
90	\centering
91	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_asymmetry_forest.pdf}
92	\caption{Transfer asymmetry ($\Delta_{\text{asym}}$) with bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n = 2{,}000$) for conflict and XNLI tasks. The conflict and XNLI CIs overlap substantially for both models, indicating that the ZH$\to$EN advantage is a general cross-lingual property. Grey band: $\pm$2~pp equivalence zone.}
93	\label{fig:asymmetry_forest}
94	\end{figure}
95	
96	\subsection{Confound Exclusion}
97	\label{sec:confounds}
98	
99	Two potential confounds could inflate cross-lingual probe accuracy: translation quality and lexical overlap between English and Chinese inputs.
100	
101	\paragraph{Translation quality.}
102	We stratify test samples by COMET score into terciles (high, medium, low) and compare EN$\to$ZH probe accuracy across strata.
103	If translation quality drove probe transfer, high-COMET samples would yield higher accuracy.
104	Table~\ref{tab:confound} shows no such pattern: Qwen exhibits a reversed direction ($\Delta = -5.6$ pp, Fisher $p = 0.695$), and Llama shows a negligible effect ($\Delta = +1.7$ pp, Fisher $p = 0.761$).
105	Translation quality does not confound the cross-lingual transfer results.
106	
107	\paragraph{Token overlap.}
108	Per-sample Jaccard similarity between English and Chinese tokenized inputs averages 3--4\%, consistent with the minimal lexical overlap between the two languages.
109	Neither model shows a positive correlation between token overlap and probe accuracy: Qwen yields no significant correlation ($p > 0.4$), and Llama shows a weak negative correlation ($r = -0.15$, $p = 0.012$), where higher overlap is associated with \emph{lower} accuracy.
110	Shared tokens do not drive cross-lingual transfer.
111	
112	\begin{table}[t]
113	\centering
114	\caption{Confound exclusion analysis. COMET tercile: $\Delta$ = balanced accuracy difference between high and low COMET score terciles for EN$\to$ZH probes (Fisher's exact test). Token overlap: Pearson correlation between per-sample Jaccard similarity and EN$\to$ZH probe accuracy.}
115	\label{tab:confound}
116	\begin{tabular}{llrl}
117	\toprule
118	Confound & Model & Result & $p$ \\
119	\midrule
120	COMET tercile & Qwen & $\Delta = -5.6$ pp & 0.695 \\
121	COMET tercile & Llama & $\Delta = +1.7$ pp & 0.761 \\
122	\midrule
123	Token overlap & Qwen & n.s. & $>$0.4 \\
124	Token overlap & Llama & $r = -0.15$ & 0.012 \\
125	\bottomrule
126	\end{tabular}
127	\end{table}
128	
129	\subsection{Causal Evidence via Representation Steering}
130	\label{sec:steering}
131	
132	The probing results establish that conflict signals are detectable, but detection does not imply causal relevance.
133	We test whether the probe weight direction $\mathbf{w}_{\text{probe}}$ causally influences conflict resolution by injecting $\alpha \cdot \mathbf{w}_{\text{probe}}$ into the last-token residual stream at a target layer during inference.
134	We measure the flip rate (fraction of samples that switch answer category) and compare against a random-direction control of equal magnitude via Fisher's exact test.
135	
136	\paragraph{L13 (mid-layer, 41\% depth).}
137	Steering at layer 13 produces significantly more behavioral change than random controls in both directions (Table~\ref{tab:steering}).
138	Positive steering ($\alpha = +5$) yields a flip rate of 19.9\% versus 6.6\% for the random control (Fisher $p = 0.002$), with all 4 directional flips moving from parametric to contextual answers.
139	Negative steering ($\alpha = -20$) yields 36.0\% versus 17.6\% (Fisher $p < 0.001$), with 5 of 6 directional flips moving from contextual to parametric.
140	Both results survive Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons.
141	The bidirectionality confirms that the probe weight vector encodes a meaningful axis of conflict resolution: positive perturbations push toward contextual answers, and negative perturbations push toward parametric answers.
142	Steering all token positions at L13 yields flip counts identical to last-token-only injection, confirming that the causal conflict signal is concentrated at the final token position rather than distributed across the sequence.
143	
144	\paragraph{Negative layers.}
145	Steering at L28 (the probing peak layer, 88\% depth) produces no significant effect with either last-token or all-position injection (all Fisher $p > 0.3$; Table~\ref{tab:steering}).
146	L18 (the MLP probe peak, 56\% depth) also shows no causal influence (all Fisher $p > 0.44$).
147	
148	\paragraph{$\alpha = +20$ reversal.}
149	At extreme perturbation magnitude ($\alpha = +20$), the control condition produces more flips than the steering condition ($p = 0.053$).
150	This indicates coherence collapse at large perturbation magnitudes, constraining the effective steering range.
151	
152	\paragraph{Detection $\neq$ Decision.}
153	The detectable conflict signal peaks at late layers (L28, 88\% depth), but causal influence on conflict resolution is localized to the mid-layer (L13, 41\% depth).
154	Probing accuracy and causal relevance thus identify different computational stages: late layers encode the outcome of conflict resolution in a linearly readable form, while mid-layers host the computation that determines the outcome.
155	
156	\paragraph{Cross-model note.}
157	The analogous Qwen steering experiments at L14 (the ZH$\to$ZH probe peak, 39\% depth) and L23 (64\% depth) did not reach significance at any $\alpha$ value (best Fisher $p = 0.682$ at L14; all $p = 1.0$ at L23), with zero directional flips (p$\to$c or c$\to$p) across all conditions (Table~\ref{tab:steering}).
158	However, this test was substantially underpowered: only 9.5\% of Qwen test samples resolved to the parametric answer at baseline ($n = 8$), compared to 23.5\% for Llama ($n = 32$), leaving insufficient parametric-class samples to detect directional shifts.
159	The non-significant Qwen result is therefore uninformative about whether the mid-layer causal effect generalizes across architectures; a balanced test set would be required to draw conclusions.
160	
161	\begin{table}[t]
162	\centering
163	\caption{Representation steering results. Flip rate: fraction of test samples switching answer category. Control: random-direction perturbation of equal magnitude. Significant results ($p < 0.05$) in bold.$^{\dagger}$}
164	\label{tab:steering}
165	\small
166	\begin{tabular}{lcrrrrl}
167	\toprule
168	Layer & Depth & $\alpha$ & Steer (\%) & Ctrl (\%) & Fisher $p$ & Dir. \\
169	\midrule
170	\textbf{L13} & \textbf{41\%} & $\mathbf{+5}$ & \textbf{19.9} & \textbf{6.6} & $\mathbf{0.002}$ & \textbf{4 p$\to$c} \\
171	\textbf{L13} & \textbf{41\%} & $\mathbf{-20}$ & \textbf{36.0} & \textbf{17.6} & $\mathbf{<0.001}$ & \textbf{5 c$\to$p} \\
172	L18 & 56\% & $+2$ & 3.7 & 1.5 & 0.447 & \\
173	L28 & 88\% & $-20$ & 14.7 & 16.9 & 0.740 & \\
174	L28 (all) & 88\% & $-20$ & 11.8 & 11.8 & 1.000 & \\
175	\midrule
176	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Qwen3-8B ($n_{\text{test}} = 84$; 9.5\% parametric baseline)}} \\
177	L14 & 39\% & $-10$ & \multicolumn{2}{c}{n.s.} & 0.682 & 0 dir. \\
178	L23 & 64\% & best & \multicolumn{2}{c}{n.s.} & 1.000 & 0 dir. \\
179	\bottomrule
180	\end{tabular}
181	\begin{flushleft}
182	\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$ At $\alpha = +20$, the control flip rate exceeds steering ($p = 0.053$), indicating coherence collapse at extreme perturbation magnitudes.
183	\end{flushleft}
184	\end{table}
```

\\\\\n\\midrule\nCOMET tercile & Qwen & $\\Delta = -5.6$ pp & 0.695 \\\\\nCOMET tercile & Llama & $\\Delta = +1.7$ pp & 0.761 \\\\\n\\midrule\nToken overlap & Qwen & n.s. & $>$0.4 \\\\\nToken overlap & Llama & $r = -0.15$ & 0.012 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Causal Evidence via Representation Steering}\n\\label{sec:steering}\n\nThe probing results establish that conflict signals are detectable, but detection does not imply causal relevance.\nWe test whether the probe weight direction $\\mathbf{w}_{\\text{probe}}$ causally influences conflict resolution by injecting $\\alpha \\cdot \\mathbf{w}_{\\text{probe}}$ into the last-token residual stream at a target layer during inference.\nWe measure the flip rate (fraction of samples that switch answer category) and compare against a random-direction control of equal magnitude via Fisher's exact test.\n\n\\paragraph{L13 (mid-layer, 41\\% depth).}\nSteering at layer 13 produces significantly more behavioral change than random controls in both directions (Table~\\ref{tab:steering}).\nPositive steering ($\\alpha = +5$) yields a flip rate of 19.9\\% versus 6.6\\% for the random control (Fisher $p = 0.002$), with all 4 directional flips moving from parametric to contextual answers.\nNegative steering ($\\alpha = -20$) yields 36.0\\% versus 17.6\\% (Fisher $p < 0.001$), with 5 of 6 directional flips moving from contextual to parametric.\nBoth results survive Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons.\nThe bidirectionality confirms that the probe weight vector encodes a meaningful axis of conflict resolution: positive perturbations push toward contextual answers, and negative perturbations push toward parametric answers.\nSteering all token positions at L13 yields flip counts identical to last-token-only injection, confirming that the causal conflict signal is concentrated at the final token position rather than distributed across the sequence.\n\n\\paragraph{Negative layers.}\nSteering at L28 (the probing peak layer, 88\\% depth) produces no significant effect with either last-token or all-position injection (all Fisher $p > 0.3$; Table~\\ref{tab:steering}).\nL18 (the MLP probe peak, 56\\% depth) also shows no causal influence (all Fisher $p > 0.44$).\n\n\\paragraph{$\\alpha = +20$ reversal.}\nAt extreme perturbation magnitude ($\\alpha = +20$), the control condition produces more flips than the steering condition ($p = 0.053$).\nThis indicates coherence collapse at large perturbation magnitudes, constraining the effective steering range.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection $\\neq$ Decision.}\nThe detectable conflict signal peaks at late layers (L28, 88\\% depth), but causal influence on conflict resolution is localized to the mid-layer (L13, 41\\% depth).\nProbing accuracy and causal relevance thus identify different computational stages: late layers encode the outcome of conflict resolution in a linearly readable form, while mid-layers host the computation that determines the outcome.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model note.}\nThe analogous Qwen steering experiments at L14 (the ZH$\\to$ZH probe peak, 39\\% depth) and L23 (64\\% depth) did not reach significance at any $\\alpha$ value (best Fisher $p = 0.682$ at L14; all $p = 1.0$ at L23), with zero directional flips (p$\\to$c or c$\\to$p) across all conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:steering}).\nHowever, this test was substantially underpowered: only 9.5\\% of Qwen test samples resolved to the parametric answer at baseline ($n = 8$), compared to 23.5\\% for Llama ($n = 32$), leaving insufficient parametric-class samples to detect directional shifts.\nThe non-significant Qwen result is therefore uninformative about whether the mid-layer causal effect generalizes across architectures; a balanced test set would be required to draw conclusions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Representation steering results. Flip rate: fraction of test samples switching answer category. Control: random-direction perturbation of equal magnitude. Significant results ($p < 0.05$) in bold.$^{\\dagger}$}\n\\label{tab:steering}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Depth & $\\alpha$ & Steer (\\%) & Ctrl (\\%) & Fisher $p$ & Dir. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{L13} & \\textbf{41\\%} & $\\mathbf{+5}$ & \\textbf{19.9} & \\textbf{6.6} & $\\mathbf{0.002}$ & \\textbf{4 p$\\to$c} \\\\\n\\textbf{L13} & \\textbf{41\\%} & $\\mathbf{-20}$ & \\textbf{36.0} & \\textbf{17.6} & $\\mathbf{<0.001}$ & \\textbf{5 c$\\to$p} \\\\\nL18 & 56\\% & $+2$ & 3.7 & 1.5 & 0.447 & \\\\\nL28 & 88\\% & $-20$ & 14.7 & 16.9 & 0.740 & \\\\\nL28 (all) & 88\\% & $-20$ & 11.8 & 11.8 & 1.000 & \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Qwen3-8B ($n_{\\text{test}} = 84$; 9.5\\% parametric baseline)}} \\\\\nL14 & 39\\% & $-10$ & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{n.s.} & 0.682 & 0 dir. \\\\\nL23 & 64\\% & best & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{n.s.} & 1.000 & 0 dir. \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\begin{flushleft}\n\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$ At $\\alpha = +20$, the control flip rate exceeds steering ($p = 0.053$), indicating coherence collapse at extreme perturbation magnitudes.\n\\end{flushleft}\n\\end{table}",

```text
72	
73	If the ZH$\to$EN transfer advantage were specific to knowledge conflicts, it should be absent or substantially reduced in a non-conflict semantic task.
74	We test this prediction using XNLI entailment-versus-contradiction probes.
75	Both models achieve high within-language accuracy on XNLI (Llama 94.0\% EN, 87.5\% ZH; Qwen 96.6\% EN, 89.5\% ZH), confirming that the probing pipeline captures semantic distinctions beyond knowledge conflicts.

77	Both XNLI CIs exclude zero, and both overlap substantially with the corresponding conflict-task CIs (Figure~\ref{fig:asymmetry_forest}).
78	The close match in asymmetry magnitude between conflict probes (Llama +7.2, Qwen +7.2 pp) and XNLI probes (Llama +6.0, Qwen +6.7 pp) indicates that the ZH$\to$EN transfer advantage is not specific to knowledge conflict representations.
79	Instead, it reflects a general cross-lingual property: Chinese-trained probes transfer to English more readily than the reverse, regardless of the downstream task.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BBwD18UNzmwBLANjhV8cJZ`</summary>

```text
120	We measure the flip rate (fraction of samples that switch answer category) and compare against a random-direction control of equal magnitude via Fisher's exact test.
121	
122	\paragraph{L13 (mid-layer, 41\% depth).}
123	Steering at layer 13 produces significantly more behavioral change than random controls in both directions (Table~\ref{tab:steering}).
124	Positive steering ($\alpha = +5$) yields a flip rate of 19.9\% versus 6.6\% for the random control (Fisher $p = 0.002$), with all 4 directional flips moving from parametric to contextual answers.
125	Negative steering ($\alpha = -20$) yields 36.0\% versus 17.6\% (Fisher $p < 0.001$), with 5 of 6 directional flips moving from contextual to parametric.
126	Both results survive Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons.
127	The bidirectionality confirms that the probe weight vector encodes a meaningful axis of conflict resolution: positive perturbations push toward contextual answers, and negative perturbations push toward parametric answers.
128	
129	\paragraph{Negative layers.}
130	Steering at L28 (the probing peak layer, 88\% depth) produces no significant effect with either last-token or all-position injection (all Fisher $p > 0.3$; Table~\ref{tab:steering}).
131	L18 (the MLP probe peak, 56\% depth) also shows no causal influence (all Fisher $p > 0.44$).
132	
133	\paragraph{$\alpha = +20$ reversal.}
134	At extreme perturbation magnitude ($\alpha = +20$), the control condition produces more flips than the steering condition ($p = 0.053$).
135	This indicates coherence collapse at large perturbation magnitudes, constraining the effective steering range.
136	
137	\paragraph{Detection $\neq$ Decision.}
138	In Llama, the detectable conflict signal peaks at late layers (L28, 88\% depth), but causal influence on conflict resolution is localized to the mid-layer (L13, 41\% depth).
139	Probing accuracy and causal relevance thus identify different computational stages: late layers encode the outcome of conflict resolution in a linearly readable form, while mid-layers host the computation that determines the outcome.
140	Injecting at all token positions (L13, all) yields identical results to last-token injection, indicating that the causal signal is concentrated in the final token's residual stream.
141	
142	\paragraph{Cross-model steering.}
143	Steering at Qwen's mid-layer (L14, 39\% depth; probe accuracy 70.2\%) produces no significant behavioral change (best Fisher $p = 0.682$, $\alpha = -10$), with zero directional flips across all steering magnitudes ($n_{\text{test}} = 84$).
144	L23 (64\% depth; probe accuracy 73.2\%) is similarly negative (all $p = 1.0$).
145	Despite comparable probe accuracies (70--73\%), the Qwen probe directions fail to causally shift answer routing, indicating that linear detectability does not entail causal relevance.
146	The mid-layer causal node identified in Llama L13 does not transfer across architectures, suggesting that the locus of conflict resolution may be model-specific even when the representational signature is shared.
147	
148	%% Table 3: Steering results
149	\begin{table}[t]
150	\centering
151	\caption{Representation steering results. Flip rate: fraction of test samples switching answer category. Control: random-direction perturbation of equal magnitude. Significant results ($p < 0.05$) in bold.$^{\dagger}$}
152	\label{tab:steering}
153	\small
154	\begin{tabular}{lcrrrrl}
155	\toprule
156	Layer & Depth & $\alpha$ & Steer (\%) & Ctrl (\%) & Fisher $p$ & Dir. \\
157	\midrule
158	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Llama-3.1-8B}} \\
159	\textbf{L13} & \textbf{41\%} & $\mathbf{+5}$ & \textbf{19.9} & \textbf{6.6} & $\mathbf{0.002}$ & \textbf{4 p$\to$c} \\
160	\textbf{L13} & \textbf{41\%} & $\mathbf{-20}$ & \textbf{36.0} & \textbf{17.6} & $\mathbf{<0.001}$ & \textbf{5 c$\to$p} \\
161	L13 (all) & 41\% & \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textit{identical to last-token}} & \\
162	L18 & 56\% & $+2$ & 3.7 & 1.5 & 0.447 & \\
163	L28 & 88\% & $-20$ & 14.7 & 16.9 & 0.740 & \\
164	L28 (all) & 88\% & $-20$ & 11.8 & 11.8 & 1.000 & \\
165	\midrule
166	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Qwen3-8B}} \\
167	L14 & 39\% & $-10$ & \multicolumn{2}{c}{0 dir.\ flips} & 0.682 & \\
168	L23 & 64\% & --- & \multicolumn{2}{c}{0 dir.\ flips} & 1.000 & \\
169	\bottomrule
170	\end{tabular}
171	\begin{flushleft}
172	\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$ At $\alpha = +20$ (Llama L13), the control flip rate exceeds steering ($p = 0.053$), indicating coherence collapse at extreme perturbation magnitudes.
173	\end{flushleft}
174	\end{table}
```

### Tool result

_source line 1892 · 2026-05-22T16:49:39.397Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01YR9tRDisgmnLirmCDL9z89`</summary>

```text
1	When multilingual large language models (LLMs) encounter conflicting information across languages, the language of presentation can determine the model's answer. Behavioral studies attribute this to linguistic affinity, but the internal mechanism remains unknown: how is the conflict signal encoded, does it share structure with general cross-lingual representations, and does detectability imply causal influence on resolution?
2	
3	We probe residual stream representations in Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B. Our analysis combines cross-lingual transfer, a non-conflict baseline on XNLI (Cross-lingual Natural Language Inference), confound exclusion for translation quality and token overlap, and representation steering. Conflict signals are linearly detectable across languages, and cross-lingual transfer exhibits a directional asymmetry: probes trained on Chinese transfer to English approximately 7 percentage points more accurately than the reverse. This asymmetry matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline in both magnitude and direction, with overlapping confidence intervals; it inherits from the model's general multilingual structure rather than conflict-specific encoding. In Llama, a dissociation emerges between detection and decision: the probe signal peaks at late layers (88\% depth), but representation steering achieves causal influence only at a mid-layer (41\% depth), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant effect. These findings demonstrate that linear detectability does not imply causal relevance, with direct implications for probe-based intervention research.
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012XwRHBRhAeMAFxCjZxV2by`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our study has three main limitations.
5	First, we evaluate only one language pair (English-Chinese) and two model architectures (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B).
6	While the consistent patterns across these two architecturally distinct models are encouraging, typologically diverse language pairs (e.g., English-German, English-Japanese) and additional model families are needed to assess the generality of our findings.
7	Second, the causal steering effect is confirmed only in Llama.
8	The Qwen steering test did not reach significance, but was underpowered due to class imbalance: only 9.5\% of Qwen test samples resolved to the parametric answer at baseline (8 samples), compared to 23.5\% for Llama (32 samples).
9	Until the detection-decision dissociation is confirmed in a second architecture with balanced test data, its generality remains provisional.
10	Third, the effective dataset size (966 to 1,368 samples per model after parametric knowledge verification) is constrained by the requirement that each model must correctly answer the question without context.
11	This verification step is necessary to ensure genuine knowledge conflicts, but it limits the statistical power of our analyses, particularly for stratified comparisons such as the COMET tercile ablation.
12	
```

</details>

For the Limitations section, I need to replace those three lines with the Director's phrasing: the steering effect was only significant in Llama at layer 13, while Qwen showed no significant effects at layers 14 and 23 despite having comparable probe accuracy. I'm replacing those three sentences with the Director's more concise version that directly states the negative result for Qwen while keeping the enumeration structure intact. The new text compresses the steering limitation into a single sentence that specifies both the successful case (Llama at L13) and the failed case (Qwen at L14 and L23), noting that despite comparable probe accuracy, the effect wasn't significant—which limits the generality claim to just one architecture so far.

```text
For the abstract, I need to decide whether to add a Qwen qualifier to the dissociation statement. The current text already specifies "In Llama," which addresses the Director's concern about qualification. Adding an explicit note about Qwen's null result would make it more complete, but abstracts need to stay tight. The real tension is whether readers might overinterpret the Llama finding as general without that caveat. I'm adding a sentence about Qwen steering results to make the cross-model limitation explicit, which directly addresses the Director's concern about clarifying that the steering findings are specific to Llama.
```

```json
    "newString": "and all late-layer interventions produce no significant effect. Analogous Qwen steering at comparable layers yields no significant behavioral change. These findings demonstrate",
    "originalFile": "When multilingual large language models (LLMs) encounter conflicting information across languages, the language of presentation can determine the model's answer. Behavioral studies attribute this to linguistic affinity, but the internal mechanism remains unknown: how is the conflict signal encoded, does it share structure with general cross-lingual representations, and does detectability imply causal influence on resolution?\n\nWe probe residual stream representations in Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B. Our analysis combines cross-lingual transfer, a non-conflict baseline on XNLI (Cross-lingual Natural Language Inference), confound exclusion for translation quality and token overlap, and representation steering. Conflict signals are linearly detectable across languages, and cross-lingual transfer exhibits a directional asymmetry: probes trained on Chinese transfer to English approximately 7 percentage points more accurately than the reverse. This asymmetry matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline in both magnitude and direction, with overlapping confidence intervals; it inherits from the model's general multilingual structure rather than conflict-specific encoding. In Llama, a dissociation emerges between detection and decision: the probe signal peaks at late layers (88\\% depth), but representation steering achieves causal influence only at a mid-layer (41\\% depth), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant effect. These findings demonstrate that linear detectability does not imply causal relevance, with direct implications for probe-based intervention research.\n",

      {
        "oldStart": 1,

          "+We probe residual stream representations in Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>
```

```text
Let me analyze all the Qwen steering mentions:

**Main paper files (used by compilation):**

1. `experiments.tex:157` — "The analogous Qwen steering experiments at L14...did not reach significance at any α value (best Fisher p = 0.682 at L14; all p = 1.0 at L23), with zero directional flips" ✅ Correctly reports negative results

2. `conclusion.tex:14` — "Qwen steering at L14 (the ZH→ZH peak) and L23 yielded no significant effects, indicating that the detection-decision dissociation observed in Llama does not straightforwardly transfer across architectures." ✅ Correctly reports negative results
   
3. `discussion.tex:12` — "The analogous Qwen experiments at L14 and L23 produced no significant steering effect and zero directional flips." ✅ Correctly reports negative results

4. `limitations.tex:7` — "Second, steering succeeded only in Llama at L13; Qwen L14 and L23 steering yielded no significant effects despite comparable probe accuracy (70--73%), limiting the detection-decision dissociation to one of two architectures tested." ✅ Just fixed

5. `abstract.tex` — Now has "Analogous Qwen steering at comparable layers yields no significant behavioral change." ✅ Just fixed

All five locations consistently report that Qwen steering experiments failed to produce significant effects, which accurately reflects the actual experimental outcomes.

6. `introduction.tex:20` — "residual stream probing with representation steering across two models" — this just says the pipeline was applied to both models, doesn't make a claim about results ✅ OK

7. `conclusion.tex:13,15` — "Several directions remain open...Future work should explore finer-grained layer sweeps and alternative steering methods, evaluate typologically diverse language pairs..." — This is about FUTURE work directions (finer-grained sweeps, other language pairs), not about Qwen steering being future work. ✅ OK

8. `discussion.tex:14` — "Until a balanced replication is conducted, cross-model generalization...remains open." ✅ This correctly frames the Qwen result as inconclusive (not "in progress"), acknowledging the experiment was done but underpowered.

9. `discussion.tex:38` — "Extending the steering analysis with balanced test sets across additional architectures..." ✅ This is future work, not claiming Qwen steering is still in progress.

The remaining sections properly distinguish between completed experiments with negative results and genuinely open future directions, so there's no misleading language about work status.

Now I need to update the stale copies in `sections/limitations.tex` and `sections/abstract.tex` to match the corrected main files, then verify the conclusion's future work mentions stay appropriately scoped to extending beyond the current work rather than completing it.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NXJudtANtndhKe8QDdFirB`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our study has three main limitations.
5	First, we evaluate only one language pair (English-Chinese) and two model architectures (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B).
6	While the consistent patterns across these two architecturally distinct models are encouraging, typologically diverse language pairs (e.g., English-German, English-Japanese) and additional model families are needed to assess the generality of our findings.
7	Second, steering succeeded only in Llama at L13; Qwen L14 and L23 steering yielded no significant effects despite comparable probe accuracy (70--73\%), limiting the detection-decision dissociation to one of two architectures tested.
8	Third, the effective dataset size (966 to 1,368 samples per model after parametric knowledge verification) is constrained by the requirement that each model must correctly answer the question without context.
9	This verification step is necessary to ensure genuine knowledge conflicts, but it limits the statistical power of our analyses, particularly for stratified comparisons such as the COMET tercile ablation.
10	
```

### Tool result

_source line 1927 · 2026-05-22T16:50:58.809Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UaHStQ8g7neUyxELcvhfSh`</summary>

```text
1	When multilingual large language models (LLMs) encounter conflicting information across languages, the language of presentation can determine the model's answer. Behavioral studies attribute this to linguistic affinity, but the internal mechanism remains unknown: how is the conflict signal encoded, does it share structure with general cross-lingual representations, and does detectability imply causal influence on resolution?
2	
3	We probe residual stream representations in Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B. Our analysis combines cross-lingual transfer, a non-conflict baseline on XNLI (Cross-lingual Natural Language Inference), confound exclusion for translation quality and token overlap, and representation steering. Conflict signals are linearly detectable across languages, and cross-lingual transfer exhibits a directional asymmetry: probes trained on Chinese transfer to English approximately 7 percentage points more accurately than the reverse. This asymmetry matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline in both magnitude and direction, with overlapping confidence intervals; it inherits from the model's general multilingual structure rather than conflict-specific encoding. In Llama, a dissociation emerges between detection and decision: the probe signal peaks at late layers (88\% depth), but representation steering achieves causal influence only at a mid-layer (41\% depth), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant effect. Analogous Qwen steering at comparable layers yields no significant behavioral change. These findings demonstrate that linear detectability does not imply causal relevance, with direct implications for probe-based intervention research.
4	
```

</details>

Analogous Qwen steering at comparable layers yields no significant behavioral change. These findings",
```json
    "newString": "A dissociation emerges between detection and decision: in Llama, the probe signal peaks at late layers (88\\% depth), but representation steering achieves causal influence only at a mid-layer (41\\% depth); analogous Qwen steering yields no significant effect. These findings",
    "originalFile": "When multilingual large language models (LLMs) encounter conflicting information across languages, the language of presentation can determine the model's answer. Behavioral studies attribute this to linguistic affinity, but the internal mechanism remains unknown: how is the conflict signal encoded, does it share structure with general cross-lingual representations, and does detectability imply causal influence on resolution?\n\nWe probe residual stream representations in Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B. Our analysis combines cross-lingual transfer, a non-conflict baseline on XNLI (Cross-lingual Natural Language Inference), confound exclusion for translation quality and token overlap, and representation steering. Conflict signals are linearly detectable across languages, and cross-lingual transfer exhibits a directional asymmetry: probes trained on Chinese transfer to English approximately 7 percentage points more accurately than the reverse. This asymmetry matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline in both magnitude and direction, with overlapping confidence intervals; it inherits from the model's general multilingual structure rather than conflict-specific encoding. In Llama, a dissociation emerges between detection and decision: the probe signal peaks at late layers (88\\% depth), but representation steering achieves causal influence only at a mid-layer (41\\% depth), and all late-layer interventions produce no significant effect. Analogous Qwen steering at comparable layers yields no significant behavioral change. These findings demonstrate that linear detectability does not imply causal relevance, with direct implications for probe-based intervention research.\n",

These findings demonstrate that linear detectability does not imply causal relevance, with direct implications for probe-based intervention research.",
          "+We probe residual stream representations in Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B. Our analysis combines cross-lingual transfer, a non-conflict baseline on XNLI (Cross-lingual Natural Language Inference), confound exclusion for translation quality and token overlap, and representation steering. Conflict signals are linearly detectable across languages, and cross-lingual transfer exhibits a directional asymmetry: probes trained on Chinese transfer to English approximately 7 percentage points more accurately than the reverse. This asymmetry matches the XNLI non-conflict baseline in both magnitude and direction, with overlapping confidence intervals; it inherits from the model's general multilingual structure rather than conflict-specific encoding. A dissociation emerges between detection and decision: in Llama, the probe signal peaks at late layers (88\\% depth), but representation steering achieves causal influence only at a mid-layer (41\\% depth); analogous Qwen steering yields no significant effect. These findings demonstrate that linear detectability does not imply causal relevance, with direct implications for probe-based intervention research."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },
```
